Reading the week
DII buying accelerated sharply this week (+₹17,316 Cr, nearly double the prior week's record) while FII flipped back to net selling (-₹1,601 Cr) — the biggest divergence between the two flows seen in this report. Despite that FII selling, Monday's heavy DII buying (+₹5,101 Cr) came on the same day as the sharpest FII selling (-₹2,535 Cr), suggesting DIIs were directly absorbing FII supply rather than the two acting independently.

06

Executive Summary — Where Is Institutional Money Flowing?

The big picture
DII buying reached a new high for this report just as FII turned net seller — and equities stabilized rather than fell further. DII bought a record +₹17,316 Cr this week (nearly double the prior record), while FII flipped to net selling (-₹1,601 Cr) after two positive weeks. Despite that FII reversal, Nifty 50's decline moderated to -0.47% from last week's -0.83%, and breadth nearly balanced out (759 Adv/808 Dec vs last week's clearly negative 635/931) — DII buying looks to be directly cushioning FII outflows rather than the two moving independently, most visible on Monday when the heaviest FII selling (-₹2,535 Cr) coincided with the heaviest DII buying (+₹5,101 Cr) of the week.

Several sections went dark this week — Derivatives, FX, Commodities and Debt weren't refreshed, since no data source was provided or searched this cycle (the reader-supplied-data-first approach now used for FII/DII wasn't extended to these). Rather than carry forward stale numbers as if current, each section explicitly flags itself as not updated. This is worth a decision going forward: either resume web search for these four sections, or find reader-side data sources for them the same way FII/DII and India stocks now have.

India stock-level data tells a stabilizing story: DMA200 crossovers were nearly flat (54 above vs 49 below, net +5) and DMA50 crossovers were close to flat too (113 vs 117, net -4) — a pause after last week's clear deterioration, not a renewed decline. The 52-week-high list grew slightly (145→154 names), consistent with the improving breadth.

Globally, this week's clearest signal came from metals: gold, silver, and copper miners all posted double-digit weekly gains in the new Global ETF tracking, a much sharper and more concentrated move than anything in the regional country ETFs. Worth watching whether this is a one-week spike or the start of a trend now that a real week-over-week comparison exists for this data.

What to watch next week
Whether FII selling extends into a third week or reverses again given how choppy the last 3 weeks have been; whether the metals-ETF strength (gold/silver/copper miners) persists into a second week; and resolving the Macros-tab data gaps (Derivatives/FX/Commodities/Debt) one way or another so the report doesn't keep carrying forward stale context indefinitely.

Global ETF Heatmap — Weekly Analysis

Executive Summary — Weekly Change

The big picture
Commodity-linked ETFs dominated this week, and breadth was strongly positive overall — 35 of 47 ETFs positive, only 12 negative. Metals miners swept the top of the leaderboard: silver miners (+11.17%), copper/metals mining (+10.77%), and copper miners (+10.37%) all posted double-digit weekly gains, with gold miners (+14.29%) the single best performer. This is a distinct, metals-led move rather than a broad regional rally — Europe (16 constituents, the largest region this week) averaged only +0.66%, and Middle East was the only region with a negative average (-1.23%), led down by Qatar (-3.43%).
